Boundary states of a bulk gapped ground state in 2 2 -D quantum spin systems
Yoshiko Ogata, Kyoto 606-8502 JAPAN

Abstract
We introduce a natural mathematical definition of boundary states of a bulk gapped ground state in the operator algebraic framework of 2 2 -D quantum spin systems. With the approximate Haag duality at the boundary, we derive a C ∗ C^{*} -tensor category ℳ ~ \tilde{\mathcal{M}} out of such boundary state. Under a non-triviality condition of the braiding in the bulk, we show that the Drinfeld center (with an asymptotic constraint) of ℳ ~ \tilde{\mathcal{M}} is equivalent to the bulk braided C ∗ C^{*} -tensor category derived in [ 26 ] .



1
Introduction

Since the discovery of Kitaev’s beautiful model
[
13
]
, the classification problem of topologically ordered gapped
systems has attracted
a lot of attention.
Ingenious models were made
[
17
]
, and
a lot of categorical arguments were carried out.
On the other hand, Naaijkens carried out an analysis of
Kitaev quantum double models from the operator algebraic/ algebraic quantum field theoretic (DHR theory)
point of view
[
22
]
,
[
21
]
,
[
6
]
,
[
23
]
.
The advantage of this direction is that DHR theory allows us to
treat non-solvable models, hence it allows us to think of the classification.
In
[
26
]
, we derived braided
C^{*}
-tensor categories out of
gapped ground states on two-dimensional quantum spin systems
satisfying the approximate Haag duality.
There, following the DHR theory
[
4
]
,
[
5
]
,
[
7
]
,
[
8
]
,
[
19
]

our objects are representations satisfying the superselection criterions.
In the same paper, we showed that this braided
C^{*}
-tensor category is an
invariant of the classification of gapped ground state phases.
This can be regarded as an analytical realization of the statement
that
anyons are invariant of topological phases
.
In particular, the existence of anyons means the ground state is
long-range entangled
[
24
]
, as was shown in
[
9
]

for a class of commuting Hamiltonians.

Bravyi and Kitaev introduced two gapped boundaries of the toric code model
[
3
]
.
In
[
14
]
, Kitaev and Kong studied the model from the categorical point of view.
There, Kitaev and Kong conjectured that the bulk theory is the Drinfeld center of the
gapped boundary theory, and “physically proved” it
for all 2+1D Levin-Wen models.
It is also stated in
\rm Theorem^{ph}
4.2.20 of
[
16
]
.
Recently, Wallick
[
28
]
analyzed the corresponding theory
in the toric code model, in terms of superselection sectors, following the corresponding bulk analysis
by Naaijkens
[
22
]
[
21
]
.
There, he showed the boundary theory is a module tensor category over the bulk.

Although it is a broadly studied subject, as far as we know, there is no mathematical formulation to specify what
a boundary state of a bulk gapped ground state
means in the operator algebraic framework.
In this paper, we suggest a natural mathematical definition of it. See Definition
1.1
.
Physically, a pure state
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}

on the half-plane is a boundary state of a bulk gapped ground state
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
if
they are macroscopically the same away from the boundary.
We analyze such a boundary state
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
in this paper.
We derive a
C^{*}
-tensor category
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}

for
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}

under the boundary version of approximate Haag duality.
We then construct a Drinfeld center
{\mathcal{Z}}_{a}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}\right)

of
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
, with some asymptotic constraint (Definition
6.9
).
We show that

{\mathcal{Z}}_{a}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}\right)
is a braided
C^{*}
-tensor category equivalent to the bulk braided
C^{*}
-tensor category
derived in
[
26
]
,
if the braiding is nontrivial in a certain sense (Assumption
6.11
and Remark
6.12
).
If we interpret our nontriviality condition related to gapped boundary condition, it gives a proof for the Kitaev and Kong’s conjecture
[
14
]
,
[
16
]
, in our setting.

1.1
Setting

Throughout this paper, we fix some
2\leq d\in\mathbb{N}
.
We denote the algebra of
d\times d
matrices by
\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\nolimits_{d}
.
For each
z\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, let
{\mathcal{A}}_{\{z\}}
be an isomorphic copy of
\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\nolimits_{d}
, and for any finite subset
\Lambda\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, we set
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda}=\bigotimes_{z\in\Lambda}{\mathcal{A}}_{\{z\}}
.
For finite
\Lambda
, the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda}
can be regarded as the set of all bounded operators acting on
the Hilbert space
\bigotimes_{z\in\Lambda}{{\mathbb{C}}}^{d}
.
We use this identification freely.
If
\Lambda_{1}\subset\Lambda_{2}
, the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{1}}
is naturally embedded in
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{2}}
by tensoring its elements with the identity.
For an infinite subset
\Gamma\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
,

{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}

is given as the inductive limit of the algebras
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda}
with
\Lambda
, finite subsets of
\Gamma
.
We call
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
the quantum spin system on
\Gamma
.
For a subset
\Gamma_{1}
of
\Gamma\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
,
the algebra
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma_{1}}
can be regarded as a subalgebra of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}
.
For
\Gamma\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, with a bit of abuse of notation, we write
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma}

to denote
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma\cap{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
.
Also,
\Gamma^{c}
denotes the complement of
\Gamma
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
We denote the algebra of local elements in
\Gamma
by
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma,\rm{loc}}
,
i.e.
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Gamma,\rm{loc}}=\cup_{\Lambda\Subset\Gamma}{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda}
.
The upper half-plane
{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,\infty)
is denoted by
\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}
,
and the lower half-plane
{\mathbb{R}}\times(-\infty,0)
is denoted by
\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{D}}
.
For
each
\bm{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\varphi\in(0,\pi)
,
we set

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\bm{a},\theta,\varphi}:=

\displaystyle\left(\bm{a}+\left\{t\bm{e}_{\beta}\mid t>0,\quad\beta\in(\theta-\varphi,\theta+\varphi)\right\}\right).

where
\bm{e}_{\beta}=(\cos\beta,\sin\beta)
.
We call a set of this form a cone, and denote by
{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}
the set of all cones.
We set
\arg\Lambda_{\bm{a},\theta,\varphi}:=(\theta-\varphi,\theta+\varphi)
and
|\arg\Lambda_{\bm{a},\theta,\varphi}|=2\varphi
.
We also set
\bm{e}_{\Lambda}:=\bm{e}_{\theta}
for
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\bm{a},\theta,\varphi}
.
For
\varepsilon>0
and
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\bm{a},\theta,\varphi}
,
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}

denotes
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}=\Lambda_{\bm{a},\theta,\varphi+\varepsilon}
.
We consider the following sets of cones:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}:=\left\{\Lambda_{\bm{a},\theta,\varphi}\mid\bm{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},\;0<\theta-\varphi<\theta+\varphi<\pi\right\},\\
&{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}:=\left\{\Lambda_{(a,0),\varphi,\varphi}\mid a\in{\mathbb{R}},\;0<\varphi<\frac{\pi}{2}\right\},\\
&{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{l}:=\left\{\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}\cap\left(\Lambda_{r}\right)^{c}\mid\Lambda_{r}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}\right\}=\left\{\overline{\Lambda_{(a,0),\frac{\pi}{2}+\varphi,\frac{\pi}{2}-\varphi}}\mid a\in{\mathbb{R}},\;0<\varphi<\frac{\pi}{2}\right\},\\
&{\mathcal{C}}_{(\theta,\varphi)}:=\left\{\Lambda\mid\overline{\arg\Lambda}\cap{[\theta-\varphi,\theta+\varphi]}=\emptyset\right\}.\end{split}

(1.1)

See Figures
4
-
4
.
Note that they are upward-filtering sets with respect to the inclusion relation.
We denote by
\mathcal{PC}
the set of all pairs
(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{r0})\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}\times{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}

with
\Lambda_{0}\subset\Lambda_{r0}
.

Let
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
be a pure state on
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
with a GNS triple

({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}},\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}},\Omega_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}})
.
Let
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
be a pure state on
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}
with a GNS triple

({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}},\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}},\Omega_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}})
.
Now we introduce our definition of boundary states of a bulk state.

Definition 1.1
.

We say that
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
is a boundary state of the bulk state
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
if

\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}
and
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}
are quasi-equivalent
for any
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
.

Recall that in quantum spin systems, two quasi-equivalent states can be approximately represented by
to each other’s local perturbations.
From this point of view, this definition is telling us that
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
is a boundary state of the bulk state
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
if they are macroscopically the same away from the boundary.
Namely, if we restrict these states to the regions of the form Figure
4
or Figure
4
,
then they look macroscopically the same.
We show this property for ground states of frustration-free Hamiltonians satisfying “strict cone
TQO”. See section
7
.
Although it is not the main target of this paper, we take this definition also for the case that the boundary state has a current at the edge.

Figure 1:
\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}
for
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}

Figure 2:
\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}
for
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}

Figure 3:
{\Lambda_{l}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{l}

Figure 4:
{\Lambda_{r}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}

We set

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U(0)}:=\cup_{\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}}\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime\prime},\quad{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}:=\overline{{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U(0)}}^{n},\\
&{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U(0)}:=\cup_{\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}}\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime\prime},\quad{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}:=\overline{{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U(0)}}^{n},\\
&{\mathcal{G}}^{(0)}:=\cup_{\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}}\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda}\right)^{\prime\prime},\quad{\mathcal{G}}:=\overline{{\mathcal{G}}^{(0)}}^{n},\\
&{\mathcal{B}}_{l}^{(0)}:=\cup_{\Lambda_{l}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{l}}\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{l}}\right)^{\prime\prime},\quad{\mathcal{B}}_{l}:=\overline{{\mathcal{B}}_{l}^{(0)}}^{n},\\
&{\mathcal{B}}_{(\theta,\varphi)}:=\overline{\cup_{\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{(\theta,\varphi)}}\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda})^{\prime\prime}}^{n}.\end{split}

(1.2)

where
{\overline{\cdot}}^{n}
indicates the norm closure.
Note that
{\mathcal{G}}={\mathcal{B}}_{(\frac{3\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2})}
.
Because
{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U},{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{l},{\mathcal{C}}_{(\theta,\varphi)}
are upward-filtering sets,

{\mathcal{F}}^{U}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}
,
{\mathcal{F}}^{U}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}
,
{\mathcal{G}}
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
,

{\mathcal{B}}_{(\theta,\varphi)}
are
C^{*}
-algebras.
Note that
{\mathcal{B}}_{(\theta,\varphi)}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
are introduced in
[
26
]
and

[
28
]
respectively.
For a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{C}
, we denote by
{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{C})
the set of all unitaries in
\mathfrak{C}
.

For example, to construct
{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U(0)}
, we collect all the von Neumann algebras

\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime\prime}
corresponding to the areas
of the shape Figure
4
or Figure
4
.
Then take the norm closure and obtain
{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}
.
We regard this
C^{*}
-algebra
{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}
, the
C^{*}
-algebra
corresponding to
operations using only the bulk
.
To construct
{\mathcal{B}}_{l}^{(0)}
, we collect all the von Neumann algebras

\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{l}}}\right)^{\prime\prime}
corresponding to the areas
of the shape Figure
4
.
Then take the norm closure and obtain
{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
.
We regard this
C^{*}
-algebra
{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
, the
C^{*}
-algebra
corresponding to
operations using the left boundary
.
By definition, we can easily see

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)\subset{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U},\quad\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)\subset{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U},\quad\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\right)\subset{\mathcal{G}}.

(1.3)

Next, we introduce representations of our interest. They are versions of localized and transportable representations in the DHR-theory. One important point that is not found in the DHR-theory is we sometimes consider only certain
charge transporters (
{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda_{r}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}
etc. below).

For a representation
\rho
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
and
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}
,
we define a set of unitaries

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}:=\left\{V_{\rho\Lambda}\in{\mathcal{U}}\left({\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}\right)\mid\left.\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(V_{\rho\Lambda}\right)\circ\rho\right|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}}}=\left.\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}\right|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}}}\right\}.

(1.4)

We denote by
O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
the set of all representations
\rho
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
,
which has a non-empty
{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}
for all
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}
.
We denote by
O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}^{U}
the set of all representations
\rho
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
,
which has a non-empty
{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}
for any
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
.
Note that
O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}\subset O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}^{U}
.
For
\Lambda_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
, we denote by
O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk},\Lambda_{0}}}^{U}

the set of all
\rho\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}^{U}
such that
\rho|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{0}^{c}}}=\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{0}^{c}}}
.
(The excitation is localized in
\Lambda_{0}
.) Physically, the excitation of
\rho\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
can be moved to any cone in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
, while for

\rho\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}^{U}
, it is guaranteed only for cones in
{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
. Later in Assumption
1.14
, we will require a kind of homogeneity, namely,
O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}=O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}^{U}
: excitations which can be localized to any
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
can be localized to any cone.

For a representation
\rho
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
and
\Lambda_{r}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
,
we define sets of unitaries

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda_{r}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}:=\left\{V_{\rho\Lambda_{r}}\in{\mathcal{U}}\left({\mathcal{B}}_{l}\right)\mid\left.\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(V_{\rho\Lambda_{r}}\right)\circ\rho\right|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}=\left.\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\right|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}\right\}\\
&{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda_{r}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}U}:=\left\{V_{\rho\Lambda_{r}}\in{\mathcal{U}}\left({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}\right)\mid\left.\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(V_{\rho\Lambda_{r}}\right)\circ\rho\right|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}=\left.\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\right|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{r}^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}\right\}.\end{split}

(1.5)

We denote by
O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
the set of all representations
\rho
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
,
which has a non-empty
{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda_{r}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}
for any
\Lambda_{r}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
.
We denote by
O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
the set of all representations
\rho
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
,
which has a non-empty
{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda_{r}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}U}
for any
\Lambda_{r}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
.
Note that
O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\subset O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
.
Physically, objects in
O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
(resp.
O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
) correspond to low energy excitations
which can be localized into the area of the form Figure
4
,
with
operations using only the bulk
(resp.
operations using the left boundary
).

For
\Lambda_{r0}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
, we denote by
O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}

(resp.
O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
)
the set of all
\rho\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
(resp.
\rho\in O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
) such that
\rho|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{r0}^{c}}}=\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{r0}^{c}}}
.
The excitation is localized in
\Lambda_{r0}
.

For a representation
\rho
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
and
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
,
we define sets of unitaries

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}:=\left\{V_{{\rho}\Lambda}\in{\mathcal{U}}\left({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}\right)\mid\left.\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(V_{\rho\Lambda}\right)\circ\rho\right|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}=\left.\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\right|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}\right\}.\end{split}

(1.6)

We denote by
O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
the set of all representations
\rho
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
,
which has a non-empty
{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
for any
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
.
Physically, objects in
O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
correspond to low energy excitations
which can be localized into the area of the form Figure
4
and Figuare
4
,
with
operations using only the bulk
.
For
\Lambda_{0}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
, we denote by

O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
the set of all
\rho\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
such that

\rho|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{0}^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}=\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{0}^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}
.
(The excitation is localized in
\Lambda_{0}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}
.)
For any
{\Lambda_{r}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
, there exists a
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
such that

(\Lambda,{\Lambda_{r}})\in\mathcal{PC}
.
Then we have
{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}\subset{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho{\Lambda_{r}}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}
,
and we have
O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}\subset O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}

for
(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{r0})\in\mathcal{PC}
.

For each
\rho,\sigma\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}^{U}
,
the set of intertwiners between them are denoted by

\displaystyle(\rho,\sigma):=\left\{R\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}})\mid R\rho(A)=\sigma(A)R,\;\;\text{for all}\;\;A\;\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\right\}.

(1.7)

For any
\rho,\sigma\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
, we set

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\rho,\sigma)_{l}:=\left\{R\in{\mathcal{B}}_{l}\mid R\rho(A)=\sigma(A)R,\;\;\text{for all}\;\;A\;\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right\},\\
&(\rho,\sigma)_{U}:=\left\{R\in{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}\mid R\rho(A)=\sigma(A)R,\;\;\text{for all}\;\;A\;\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right\}.\end{split}

(1.8)

Note that
(\rho,\sigma)_{U}\subset(\rho,\sigma)_{l}
.

For representations
\rho,\sigma
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}
on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
, we write
\rho\simeq_{l}\sigma

(resp.
\rho\simeq_{U}\sigma
)
if there exists a unitary
U\in{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
(resp.
U\in{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
) such that
\rho=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits U\sigma
.
It is important to note that
\simeq_{l}
is coarser than
\simeq_{U}
.
Physically, we understand
\rho\simeq_{U}\sigma
means we can map
\rho
to
\sigma

using only “bulk operation”.
On the other hand,
\rho\simeq_{l}\sigma
can require “truely boundary operation” to map

\rho
to
\sigma
.

Example 1.2
.

Let us consider the smooth boundary of the Toric code studied in
[
28
]
.
For reader’s convenience, we briefly recall the morphisms introduced in
[
28
]
.
See
[
28
]
Section 6 for more detailed information.
For an infinite path
\gamma
on the dual lattice and an infinite path
\gamma^{\prime}

on the lattice,
let
\rho_{\gamma}^{X}
,
\rho_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{Z}
be the endomorphisms given by
adjoints of
products of Pauli
X
along
\gamma
, products of Pauli
Z
along
\gamma^{\prime}
respectively.
The representations
\pi_{\gamma}^{X}:=\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\rho_{\gamma}^{X}
,

\pi_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{Z}:=\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\rho_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{Z}
correspond to
m
-particle and
e
-particle, respectively.

Let
\Lambda,\Lambda_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
and
{\gamma^{\prime}},\gamma_{1}^{\prime}
be infinite paths on the lattice
in
\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}
,
\Lambda_{1}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}
respectively.
Note that there exists a cone
\Lambda_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
such that
\Lambda\cup\Lambda_{1}\subset\Lambda_{2}
.
In
[
28
]
, Wallick constructed a transporter from
\pi_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{Z}
to
\pi_{\gamma_{1}^{\prime}}^{Z}

as follows. :
Let
\zeta_{n}^{\prime}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of paths on the lattice in
\Lambda_{2}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}

from the
n
-th vertex of
\gamma^{\prime}
to the
n
-th vertex of
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}
.
We take
\zeta_{n}^{\prime}
so that the distances from
\zeta_{n}^{\prime}
to the starting sites of

\gamma^{\prime}
,
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}
go to infinity as
n\to\infty
. (See Figure
6
)
Then, as was shown in
[
28
]
, the weak limit
V
of

\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left(\Gamma_{\gamma_{n}^{\prime}}^{Z}\Gamma_{\zeta_{n}^{\prime}}^{Z}\Gamma_{\gamma_{1n}^{\prime}}^{Z}\right)

exists.
Here,
\gamma_{n}^{\prime}
and
\gamma_{1n}^{\prime}
are the first
n
-step of

\gamma^{\prime},\gamma_{1}^{\prime}
and
\Gamma_{\gamma_{n}^{\prime}}^{Z}
,

\Gamma_{\zeta_{n}^{\prime}}^{Z}
,
\Gamma_{\gamma_{1n}^{\prime}}^{Z}

are the product of Pauli
Z
along
\gamma_{n}^{\prime}
,
\zeta_{n}^{\prime}
,
\gamma_{1n}^{\prime}
.
This
V
is a unitary, and
we have
\pi_{\gamma^{\prime}_{1}}^{Z}=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits(V)\pi_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{Z}
.
Because all of
\gamma_{n}^{\prime}
,
\zeta_{n}^{\prime}
,
\gamma_{1n}^{\prime}
are in

\Lambda_{2}
, the operator

\Gamma_{\gamma_{n}^{\prime}}^{Z}\Gamma_{\zeta_{n}^{\prime}}^{Z}\Gamma_{\gamma_{1n}^{\prime}}^{Z}

belongs to
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{2}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}V={\mathop{\mathrm{w-}}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left(\Gamma_{\gamma_{n}^{\prime}}^{Z}\Gamma_{\zeta_{n}^{\prime}}^{Z}\Gamma_{\gamma_{1n}^{\prime}}^{Z}\right)\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{2}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime\prime}\subset{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}.\end{split}

(1.9)

From this and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits(V)\pi_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{Z}|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{1}^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}=\pi_{\gamma^{\prime}_{1}}^{Z}|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{1}^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}=\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{1}^{c}}},\end{split}

(1.10)

we obtain
V\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\pi_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{Z}{\Lambda}_{1}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}

In
[
28
]
, it is also shown that the
m
-particle is unitarily equivalent to the vacuum:
Let us consider an infinite path
\gamma
on the dual lattice in an intersection of
\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}

and
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
, starting at the boundary.
Note that there is a cone
\Lambda_{l}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{l}
including
\Lambda
.
Let
\gamma_{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
be the path consisting of the first
n
bonds
of
\gamma
.
Let
\zeta_{n}
be a
path on the dual lattice in
\Lambda_{l}
from the end of
\gamma_{n}
to the boundary
(a_{n},0)
.
(See Figure
6
)
We require the distance from
\zeta_{n}
to the starting point of
\gamma
goes to
infinity as
n\to\infty
. In particular,
a_{n}\to-\infty
as
n\to\infty
.
Then, as was shown in
[
28
]
, the weak limit
V
of

\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left(\Gamma_{\gamma_{n}}^{X}\Gamma_{\zeta_{n}}^{X}\right)

exists.
Here,
\Gamma_{\gamma_{n}}^{X}
,

\Gamma_{\zeta_{n}}^{X}

are the product of Pauli
X
along
\gamma_{n}
,
\zeta_{n}
.
This
V
is a unitary, and
we have
\pi_{\gamma}^{X}=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits(V)\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
.
Because both of
\gamma_{n}
,
\zeta_{n}
are in

\Lambda_{l}
, the operator

\Gamma_{\gamma_{n}}^{X}\Gamma_{\zeta_{n}}^{X}

belongs to
{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{l}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}V={\mathop{\mathrm{w-}}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left(\Gamma_{\gamma_{n}}^{X}\Gamma_{\zeta_{n}}^{X}\right)\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{l}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime\prime}\subset{\mathcal{B}}_{l}.\end{split}

(1.11)

This means
\pi_{\gamma}^{X}\simeq_{l}\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
.
Note also that this
V
belongs to
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}

because

\displaystyle\begin{split}\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits V^{*}\pi_{\gamma}^{X}|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits V^{*}\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}},\end{split}

(1.12)

since
\gamma
is inside of
\Lambda
.
An important fact is that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime\prime}\subsetneq\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\end{split}

(1.13)

by Proposition 12.7 of
[
28
]
. The right-hand side is truely bigger than the left-hand side.
Note that the left-hand side
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime\prime}

is included in
{\mathcal{F}}
.
In fact as we will see in Remark
5.3
,

\pi_{\gamma}^{X}
and
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
are not equivalent with respect to
\simeq_{\mathcal{F}}
.
In particular,
V\notin{\mathcal{F}}
and
V\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\setminus\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}
.

1.2
Assumptions

Let us consider Setting
1.1
.
We introduce our assumptions in this section.
First of all, we consider the situation that
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
is a boundary state of
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
.

Assumption 1.3
.

The state
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
is a boundary state of
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
.
Namely, for any
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
, representations

\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}
and
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}|_{{\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}}
are quasi-equivalent.

In the DHR theory, the Haag duality is assumed.
In the quantum spin setting, the Haag duality is defined as follows:
a representation
\pi
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}
satisfies the Haag duality if

\displaystyle\pi\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}}\right)^{\prime}=\pi\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda}\right)^{\prime\prime}

(1.14)

for any cone
\Lambda
.

Note that
\pi({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda})^{\prime\prime}\subset\pi\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}}\right)^{\prime}
always holds.
The requirement here is that they are actually equal.

We introduced the following approximate Haag duality in
[
26
]
.
This is a relaxation of the Haag duality.

Assumption 1.4
.

Consider the setting in subsection
1.1
.
For any
\varphi\in(0,\pi)
and

\varepsilon>0
with

2\varphi+4\varepsilon<2\pi
,
there is some
R^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon}\geq 0
and decreasing
functions
f^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon,\delta}(t)
,
\delta>0

on
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}

with
\lim_{t\to\infty}f^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon,\delta}(t)=0

such that

(i)

for any cone
\Lambda
with
|\arg\Lambda|=2\varphi
, there is a unitary

U^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}})

satisfying

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}}\right)^{\prime}\subset\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(U^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\right)\left(\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\left(\Lambda-R^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon}\bm{e}_{\Lambda}\right)_{\varepsilon}}\right)^{\prime\prime}\right),

(1.15)

and

(ii)

for any
\delta>0
and
t\geq 0
, there is a unitary

\tilde{U}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\Lambda,\varepsilon,\delta,t}\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{\varepsilon+\delta}-t\bm{e}_{\Lambda}}\right)^{\prime\prime}

satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|U^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}-\tilde{U}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\Lambda,\varepsilon,\delta,t}\right\|\leq f^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon,\delta}(t).

(1.16)

Note that if the Haag duality holds, then the approximate Haag duality holds with

U^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}=\tilde{U}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\Lambda,\varepsilon,\delta,t}=\mathbb{I}

and
f^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon,\delta}(t)=0
,
R^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon}=0
.

In Figure
8
, the dark colored cone is
\Lambda
,
and the light colored cone is
{\Lambda_{\varepsilon+\delta}-t\bm{e}_{\Lambda}}
.
The unitary
\tilde{U}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\Lambda,\varepsilon,\delta,t}
is localized in the light cone area.
(i) combined with (ii) tells us the following:
unlike the Haag duality,
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}})^{\prime}
may not be equal to

\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda})^{\prime\prime}
. However, if we consider the enlarged cone

{\Lambda_{\varepsilon+\delta}-t\bm{e}_{\Lambda}}
, then

\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}})^{\prime}
is “approximately” included in
the von Neumann algebra corresponding to that cone
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{\varepsilon+\delta}-t\bm{e}_{\Lambda}}\right)^{\prime\prime}
.
More precisely, for any
a\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}})^{\prime}
,
there exists a
b\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{\varepsilon+\delta}-t\bm{e}_{\Lambda}}\right)^{\prime\prime}

with
\left\|b\right\|=\left\|a\right\|

such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\|b-a\right\|\leq 2f^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon,\delta}(t)\left\|a\right\|.\end{split}

(1.17)

The advantage of the approximate Haag duality over the Haag duality is that we know that the approximate Haag duality is stable under local unitary operations
[
26
]
.

The following Lemma is immediate from the definition.

Lemma 1.5
.

Consider the setting in subsection
1.1
and assume Assumption
1.4
.
Then for any
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
, we have

\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda^{c}}\right)^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{G}}
.

The following two assumptions are boundary version of the approximate Haag duality.

Assumption 1.6
.

For any
\varphi\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
,
\varepsilon>0
with
2\varphi+2\varepsilon<\pi
,
there is some
R^{(r){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon}>0
and decreasing
functions
f^{(r){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon,\delta}(t)
,
\delta>0

on
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}

with
\lim_{t\to\infty}f^{(r){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon,\delta}(t)=0

such that

(i)

for any cone
\Lambda_{r}=\Lambda_{(a,0),\varphi,\varphi}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
, there is a unitary

U^{(r){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\Lambda_{r},\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}})

satisfying

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{(\Lambda_{r})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\subset\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(U^{(r){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\Lambda_{r},\varepsilon}\right)\left(\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{(a-R^{(r){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon},0),\varphi+\varepsilon,\varphi+\varepsilon}}\right)^{\prime\prime}\right),

(1.18)

and

(ii)

for any
\delta>0
and
t\geq 0
, there is a unitary

\tilde{U}^{(r){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\Lambda_{r},\varepsilon,\delta,t}\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{(a-t,0),\varphi+\varepsilon+\delta,\varphi+\varepsilon+\delta}}\right)^{\prime\prime}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}

satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|U^{(r){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\Lambda_{r},\varepsilon}-\tilde{U}^{(r){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\Lambda_{r},\varepsilon,\delta,t}\right\|\leq f_{\varphi,\varepsilon,\delta}^{(r){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}(t).

(1.19)

Note in particular, we have
U^{(r){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\Lambda_{r},\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}
.

In Figure
8
, the dark-colored cone indicates
\Lambda_{r}
, and the light-colored cone
indicates
\Lambda_{(a-t,0),\varphi+\varepsilon+\delta,\varphi+\varepsilon+\delta}
.
The unitary
\tilde{U}^{(r){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\Lambda_{r},\varepsilon,\delta,t}
is localized in the light-colored cone.
The new ingredient compared to the Assumption
1.4
is that we require the unitary

\tilde{U}^{(r){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\Lambda_{r},\varepsilon,\delta,t}
to be in
{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}

, not only in
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{(a-t,0),\varphi+\varepsilon+\delta,\varphi+\varepsilon+\delta}}\right)^{\prime\prime}
.

Here is the left version.

Assumption 1.7
.

For any
\varphi\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
,
\varepsilon>0
with
2\varphi+2\varepsilon<\pi
,
there is some
R^{(l){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon}>0
and decreasing
functions
f^{(l){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon,\delta}(t)
,
\delta>0

on
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}

with
\lim_{t\to\infty}f^{(l){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon,\delta}(t)=0

such that

(i)

for any cone
\Lambda_{l}=\overline{\Lambda_{(a,0),\pi-\varphi,\varphi}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{l}
, there is a unitary

U^{(l){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\Lambda_{l},\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{U}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}})

satisfying

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{l}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\subset\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(U^{(l){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\Lambda_{l},\varepsilon}\right)\left(\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\overline{\Lambda_{(\left(a+R^{(l){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon}\right),0),\pi-(\varphi+\varepsilon),(\varphi+\varepsilon)}}}\right)^{\prime\prime}\right),

(1.20)

and

(ii)

for any
\delta>0
and
t\geq 0
, there is a unitary

\tilde{U}^{(l){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\Lambda_{l},\varepsilon,\delta,t}\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\overline{\Lambda_{(a+t,0),\pi-(\varphi+\varepsilon+\delta),(\varphi+\varepsilon+\delta)}}}\right)^{\prime\prime}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}

satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|U^{(l){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\Lambda_{l},\varepsilon}-\tilde{U}^{(l){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\Lambda_{l},\varepsilon,\delta,t}\right\|\leq f^{(l){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\varphi,\varepsilon,\delta}(t).

(1.21)

Note in particular, we have
U^{(l){\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}_{\Lambda,\varepsilon}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}
.

Remark 1.8
.

The non-approximate versions of Assumption
1.6
and Assumption
1.7
are

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{r}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{r}}}\right)^{\prime\prime},\quad{\Lambda_{r}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r},\\
&\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{l}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{l}}}\right)^{\prime\prime},\quad{\Lambda_{l}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{l},\end{split}

(1.22)

respectively.
In
[
28
]
, Wallick showed this non-approximate version for the smooth boundary of the Toric code.

In order to have direct sum and subobjects, we need the following assumption.

Assumption 1.9
.

For any
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
,
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda})^{\prime\prime}
is an infinite factor.

The boundary version is the following.

Assumption 1.10
.

For any
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
,
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}})^{\prime\prime}
is an infinite factor.

From Assumption
1.10
, we can derive infiniteness of von Neumann algebras of larger regions.

Lemma 1.11
.

Assume Assumption
1.10
.
Then for any
{\Lambda_{l}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{l}
,
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{l}}})^{\prime\prime}
is an infinite factor.

Because
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
is pure,
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{l}}})^{\prime\prime}
is a factor for any
{\Lambda_{l}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{l}
.
For any
{\Lambda_{l}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{l}
, there exists a
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
such that

\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}\subset{\Lambda_{l}}
, hence
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}})^{\prime\prime}\subset\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{l}}})^{\prime\prime}
.
Therefore, if Assumption
1.10
holds, then
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{l}}})^{\prime\prime}
is an infinite factor.

\square

We also note the following.

Lemma 1.12
.

If Assumption
1.9
and Assumption
1.3
hold,
then Assumption
1.10
holds.

This is because

\Lambda\setminus\left(\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}\right)
is finite
for any
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
.

\square

Combining this and Lemma 5.3 Lemma 5.5 of
[
26
]
, for boundary states of gapped ground states, Assumption
1.9

and Assumption
1.10
hold automatically.

Lemma 1.13
.

Suppose that
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
is a gapped ground state in the sense it satisfies
the gap inequality (5.1)of
[
26
]
, and
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
is a boundary state of
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
.
Then Assumption
1.9
and Assumption
1.10
hold.

The following assumption means that in
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
, any quasi-particles
in the upper half plane can freely move to the lower half plane.

Assumption 1.14
.

O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}^{U}=O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
.

In this paper, we consider such a homogeneous situation.

Example 1.15
.

Assumptions in this subsection are satisfied by the Toric code:
By
[

]
,
[
21
]
or with the argument there, we know that the condition
in Definition
7.1
holds for the Toric code.
From Proposition
7.3
, this guarantees Assumption
1.3

for the smooth/rough boundaries of Toric code.
Thanks to
[

and
[

1.4
,
Assumption
1.6
and Assumption
1.7
are also satisfied.
Assumption
1.9
and Assumption
1.10
can be found there as well,
although they also follow from the gap condition
[
26
]
.
Assumption
1.14
follows from the argument in
[
23
]
:
the argument following

[
12
]
already gives a bound for the number of sectors in

.
But as was shown in

]
this number is already saturated by
sectors coming from
O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
. (Recall
O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}\subset O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}^{U}
.)

1.3
Results

We consider the setting in subsection
1.1
.
The proof in
[
26
]
shows the following.

Theorem 1.16
.

Consider the setting in subsection
1.1
.
Under Assumption
1.4
and Assumption
1.9
,

O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk},\Lambda_{0}}}
with their morphisms
(\rho,\sigma)
,

\rho,\sigma\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk},\Lambda_{0}}}

forms a braided
C^{*}
-tensor category
C_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk},\Lambda_{0}}}
.

See
[
25
]
for the definition of braided
C^{*}
-tensor category and related notions.

Analogous to this theorem, we can derive various
C^{*}
-tensor categories
from our boundary state
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
. We can consider different categories
depending on our choices of objects (representations of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}
) and
morphisms (intertwiners between representations).
Under
Assumption
1.10
we can show that
all of the following categories are
C^{*}
-tensor categories
(Theorem
2.10
, Theorem
2.13
, Theorem
2.14
).

1.

The category
C^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
with objects
O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}

and morphisms
(\rho,\sigma)_{l}
between objects
\rho,\sigma\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
.

2.

The category
C^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
with objects
O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}

and morphisms
(\rho,\sigma)_{U}
between objects

\rho,\sigma\in O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
.

3.

The category
C_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
with objects
O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}

and morphisms
(\rho,\sigma)_{U}
between objects
\rho,\sigma\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
.

For each
\rho\in O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
we can define
a family of natural isomorphisms

\iota^{(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{r0})}\left(-:\rho\right):O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}\to(-\otimes\rho,\rho\otimes-)_{U}
, satisfying the half-braiding equation
(Proposition
3.11
, Lemma
3.13
, Lemma
3.16
, Lemma
3.18
),
under Assumption
1.6
Assumption
1.7
.
This
\iota^{(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{r0})}\left(-:\rho\right)
satisfies some asymptotic property (Lemma
3.20
).
When we restrict this to
O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
,

C_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
becomes braided
C^{*}
-tensor category (Theorem
3.19
).
The category
C_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
can be regarded as a center of
C^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
in a certain sense.
See Appendix
C
.

Using the condition that
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
is a boundary of
\omega_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}
(Assumption
1.3
),
we can show the following. See Corollary
5.2
for a more detailed statement.

Theorem
.

The braided
C^{*}
-tensor category
C_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
and the bulk braided
C^{*}
-tensor category

C_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk},\Lambda_{0}}}
are equivalent.

Hence at the boundary, we have a “copy” of the bulk theory.
However,
C_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
is not preferable as the boundary theory.
This is because the morphisms are too limited there, i.e.,
we allow ourselves to use only
{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}
.
In particular, as we saw in Example
1.2
for toric code
with rough boundary,
m
particle is
not
equivalent to the vacuum
with respect to

\simeq_{U}
, while
it
is
equivalent to the vacuum
with respect to

\simeq_{l}
.
Therefore, we prefer to use
{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
as morphisms.
Hence in section
6
we introduce a category
{\mathcal{M}}
with object
O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
,
and morphisms between objects
\rho,\sigma\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}

defined by

{\mathop{\mathrm{Mor}}}_{{\mathcal{M}}}(\rho,\sigma):=(\rho,\sigma)_{l}
.
The category
{\mathcal{M}}
is a tensor category.
However, it is
not
a
C^{*}
-tensor category, in the sense that we cannot
construct a subobject in general.
In order to overcome this difficulty, we introduce the idempotent completion
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}

of
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Then we obtain the following (see Proposition
6.2
).

Proposition
.

This
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
is a
C^{*}
-tensor category

We regard this
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
as our boundary theory.
For this
C^{*}
-tensor category
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
,
we introduce
the Drinfeld center with an asymptotic constraint

{\mathcal{Z}}_{a}(\tilde{\mathcal{M}})
of
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
.
This is the same as the usual Drinfeld center (Definition
6.9
), except that the half-braidings
are required to satisfy the asymptotic property satisfied by
our
\iota^{(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{r0})}
.
Now we assume that the braiding of
C_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk},\Lambda_{0}}}
is non-trivial in the following sense :
if two quasi-particles in the bulk have the asymptotically unitarily equivalent braiding, then they
are equivalent. For more precise condition, see Assumption
6.11
and Remark
6.12
.
We regard this condition as “a stable gapped boundary” condition. See Remark
6.13

for the reasoning.
Then we obtain the following.

Theorem
.

The Drinfeld center with the asymptotic constraint

{\mathcal{Z}}_{a}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{M}}\right)
is a braided
C^{*}
-tensor category equivalent to the bulk braided
C^{*}
-tensor category

C_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk},\Lambda_{0}}}
.

Example 1.17
.

From Example
1.15
, all the assumptions in this subsection are
satisfied in Toric code, and we may apply our theorem.
We will see from Example
4.8
and Theorem
6.17
that this equivalence
maps bulk objects
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}\rho_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{Z}
(with
\gamma^{\prime}
an infinite path in the upper half plane) to a boundary object

(\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\rho_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{Z},\tilde{\iota}\left((-,-),(\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\rho_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{Z},\mathbb{I})\right))

where
\tilde{\iota}
is the half-braiding introduced in Lemma
6.7
.
This recovers the equivalence shown in
[
28
]
.

1.4
Comparisons with former works

In
[
28
]
, Wallick (besides things we already mentioned about),
showed that the superselection sectors
\pi_{\gamma}^{k}
,
given by infinite paths of all types, and their direct sums form a fusion category
\Delta(\Lambda)
(section 8
[
28
]
).
In fact, as was shown in Theorem 12.9 of
[
28
]
, they are basically all the superselection sectors at the boundary. The proof of Theorem 12.9 of
[
28
]
relies on the index theory given by
[
12
]
, and requires quite a bit of information of the model.
The functor from the bulk to the boundary constructed in
[
28
]

is given by a concrete formula, using the information of the path
\gamma
.
In our general framework, it is hard to talk about the index, and there is no path

\gamma
that allows us to define the functor explicitly.
The thing we use instead to introduce our functor is
the quasi-equivalence given by Assumption
1.3
.
This allows us to prove that there is a copy of the bulk theory at the boundary
Corollary
5.2
.

Our main theorem proves that the bulk theory is the Drinfeld center of the
“stable gapped boundary” theory, in our setting of superselection sectors.
Because the formulation of
[
14
]
is
not given in terms of superselection sectors, we cannot make a direct connection
with our objects and objects studied in
[
14
]
.
However, assuming that the category given by superselection sectors (in our sense)
and that of
[
14
]
are identical, we can relate our result to that
of
[
14
]
.
The identification is non-trivial though.
There is yet another approach to boundary theory
[
10
]
, mostly focusing on systems with commuting Hamiltonians, but affording more informations for such cases. It would be interesting to see the relation between their approach and ours.
That might prove the identification we mentioned above.

2
Boundary theories

In this section, we introduce braided
C^{*}
-tensor categories

C^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
,
C^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
,
C_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
.
The proof follows the standard DHR theory
[
4
]
that is adapted to spin systems

[
26
]
at the boundary.

First, we extend each
\rho\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
to an endomorphism of
{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
.
Note, as in the standard DHR theory, we cannot extend
\rho
to the whole

\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{H_{U}})^{\prime\prime}={\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}})

\sigma
-weak continuously.
If that was possible,
\rho
would be quasi-equivalent to
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Consider the setting in subsection
1.1
.
Then for any
\rho\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
,
\Lambda_{r0}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
and
V_{\rho,\Lambda_{r0}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda_{r0}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}
,
there exists a unique
*
-homomorphism
T_{\rho}^{\mathrm{(l)}\Lambda_{r0}V_{{\rho},\Lambda_{r0}}}:{\mathcal{B}}_{l}\to{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}})

such that

(i)

T_{\rho}^{\mathrm{(l)}\Lambda_{r0}V_{{\rho},\Lambda_{r0}}}\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(V_{\rho,\Lambda_{r0}}\right)\rho
,

(ii)

T_{\rho}^{\mathrm{(l)}\Lambda_{r0}V_{{\rho},\Lambda_{r0}}}
is
\sigma
-weak continuous on

\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda_{l}})^{\prime\prime}
for all
\Lambda_{l}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{l}
.

It satisfies
T_{\rho}^{\mathrm{(l)}\Lambda_{r0}V_{{\rho},\Lambda_{r0}}}({\mathcal{B}}_{l})\subset{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
and defines an endomorphism

T_{\rho}^{\mathrm{(l)}\Lambda_{r0}V_{{\rho},\Lambda_{r0}}}:{\mathcal{B}}_{l}\to{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
.
This endomorphism also satisfies

\displaystyle\left.T_{\rho}^{\mathrm{(l)}\Lambda_{r0}V_{{\rho},\Lambda_{r0}}}\right|_{\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{(\Lambda_{r0})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime\prime}}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{(\Lambda_{r0})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime\prime}}.

(2.1)

If furthermore
\rho\in O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
and
V_{\rho,\Lambda_{r0}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda_{r0}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}U}

then we have
T_{\rho}^{\mathrm{(l)}\Lambda_{r0}V_{{\rho},\Lambda_{r0}}}({\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U})\subset{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}
.

Each
{\Lambda_{l}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{l}
can be written as
{\Lambda_{l}}=({\Lambda_{r}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}

with some
{\Lambda_{r}}=\Lambda_{(a,0),\varphi,\varphi}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
.
Set

\kappa_{{\Lambda_{l}}}:=\left\{\Gamma_{r}\mid\Gamma_{r}=\Lambda_{(b,0),\varphi_{1},\varphi_{1}},\;a<b,\;0<\varphi_{1}<\varphi\right\}\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}.

Then, as in Lemma 2.11 of
[
26
]
,
for any
\rho\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
,
\Lambda_{r0}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
and
V_{\rho,\Lambda_{r0}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda_{r0}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}
,

\displaystyle T_{\rho}^{(0)}(x):=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(V_{\rho,\Lambda_{r0}}V_{\rho,K_{{\Lambda_{l}}}}^{*}\right)(x),\quad\text{if}\;x\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{l}}}\right)^{\prime\prime},\;{\Lambda_{l}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{l}

(2.2)

defines a isometric
*
-homomorphism
T_{\rho}^{(0)}:{\mathcal{B}}_{l}^{(0)}\to{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
,
independent of the choice of
K_{{\Lambda_{l}}}
,
V_{\rho,K_{{\Lambda_{l}}}}
,
{\Lambda_{l}}
.
As in Lemma 2.13 of
[
26
]
, this
T_{\rho}^{(0)}
extends to
the
T_{\rho}^{\mathrm{(l)}\Lambda_{r0}V_{{\rho},\Lambda_{r0}}}
with the desired property.
The last statement is trivial from the definition above.

\square

Remark 2.2
.

If we consider the case
\rho\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
and
V_{\rho,\Lambda_{r0}}=\mathbb{I}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda_{r0}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}
, then this gives the extension

T_{\rho}^{\mathrm{(l)}\Lambda_{r0}\mathbb{I}}
of
\rho
.

Remark 2.3
.

We extend the equivalence relations
\simeq_{l}
,
\simeq_{U}

to endomorphisms of
{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
in an obvious way.

By the same proof as that of
[
26
]
Lemma 2.14,
for any
{\Lambda_{r1}},{\Lambda_{r2}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
,
\rho\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
,
V_{\rho,{\Lambda_{ri}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho{\Lambda_{ri}}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}
,
i=1,2
,
we have

T_{\rho}^{\mathrm{(l)}{{\Lambda_{r2}}}V_{{\rho},{{\Lambda_{r2}}}}}=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(V_{\rho,{\Lambda_{r2}}}V_{\rho,{\Lambda_{r1}}}^{*}\right)T_{\rho}^{\mathrm{(l)}{{\Lambda_{r1}}}V_{{\rho},{{\Lambda_{r1}}}}}
.
For
{\Lambda_{ri}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
,
\rho_{i}\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
(resp.
\rho_{i}\in O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
),
V_{\rho_{i},{\Lambda_{ri}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho_{i}{\Lambda_{ri}}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}

(resp.
V_{\rho_{i},{\Lambda_{ri}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho_{i}{\Lambda_{ri}}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}U}
),
i=1,2

with
\rho_{1}\simeq_{l}\rho_{2}

(resp.
\rho_{1}\simeq_{U}\rho_{2}
) we have

T_{\rho_{1}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{r1}}V_{{\rho_{1}},{\Lambda_{r1}}}}\simeq_{l}T_{\rho_{2}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{r2}}V_{{\rho_{2}},{\Lambda_{r2}}}}

(resp.
T_{\rho_{1}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{r1}}V_{{\rho_{1}},{\Lambda_{r1}}}}\simeq_{U}T_{\rho_{2}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{r2}}V_{{\rho_{2}},{\Lambda_{r2}}}}
).

The extension allows us to consider compositions, which will give us the tensor structure.

Lemma 2.4
.

Consider the setting in subsection
1.1
, and let
\Lambda_{r0}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
.
Then for any
\rho,\sigma\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
, we have

\displaystyle\rho\circ_{\Lambda_{r0}}\sigma:=T_{\rho}^{\mathrm{(l)}\Lambda_{r0}\mathbb{I}}\;T_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{(l)}\Lambda_{r0}\mathbb{I}}\;\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}},

(2.3)

and this defines a composition
\circ_{\Lambda_{r0}}:O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}\times O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}\to O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
.
This composition
\circ_{\Lambda_{r0}}
satisfies the following properties.

(i)

For
\rho,\sigma\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
, we have
T_{\rho\circ_{\Lambda_{r0}}\sigma}^{\mathrm{(l)}\Lambda_{r0}\mathbb{I}}=T_{\rho}^{\mathrm{(l)}\Lambda_{r0}\mathbb{I}}T_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{(l)}\Lambda_{r0}\mathbb{I}}
.

(ii)

For
\rho,\sigma,\gamma\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
,

\left(\rho\circ_{\Lambda_{r0}}\sigma\right)\circ_{\Lambda_{r0}}\gamma=\rho\circ_{\Lambda_{r0}}\left(\sigma\circ_{\Lambda_{r0}}\gamma\right)
.

(iii)

If
\rho,\sigma\in O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
, then
\rho\circ_{\Lambda_{r0}}\sigma\in O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
.

(iv)

If
(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{r0})\in\mathcal{PC}
and
\rho,\sigma\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
, then

{\rho\circ_{\Lambda_{r0}}\sigma}\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
.

This follows from the same proof as that of Lemma 3.3, 3.6, 3.7 of
[
26
]
,
and definition of
O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
,
O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
.

\square

Let
\rho_{i},\sigma_{i}\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
(resp
\rho_{i},\sigma_{i}\in O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
),

{\Lambda_{ri}},{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
,
V_{\rho_{i},{\Lambda_{ri}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho_{i}{\Lambda_{ri}}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}
,

V_{\sigma_{i},{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\sigma_{i}{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}

(resp
V_{\rho_{i},{\Lambda_{ri}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho_{i}{\Lambda_{ri}}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}U}
,

V_{\sigma_{i},{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\sigma_{i}{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}U}
), for
i=1,2
.
If
\rho_{1}\simeq_{l}\rho_{2}
and
\sigma_{1}\simeq_{l}\sigma_{2}

(resp.
\rho_{1}\simeq_{U}\rho_{2}
and
\sigma_{1}\simeq_{U}\sigma_{2}
)
then we have

T_{\rho_{1}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{r1}}V_{{\rho_{1}},{\Lambda_{r1}}}}T_{\sigma_{1}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{r1}}^{\prime}V_{{\sigma_{1}},{\Lambda_{r1}}^{\prime}}}\simeq_{l}T_{\rho_{2}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{2r}}V_{{\rho_{2}},{\Lambda_{2r}}}}T_{\sigma_{2}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{2r}}^{\prime}V_{{\sigma_{2}},{\Lambda_{2r}}^{\prime}}}

(resp.
T_{\rho_{1}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{r1}}V_{{\rho_{1}},{\Lambda_{r1}}}}T_{\sigma_{1}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{r1}}^{\prime}V_{{\sigma_{1}},{\Lambda_{r1}}^{\prime}}}\simeq_{U}T_{\rho_{2}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{2r}}V_{{\rho_{2}},{\Lambda_{2r}}}}T_{\sigma_{2}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{2r}}^{\prime}V_{{\sigma_{2}},{\Lambda_{2r}}^{\prime}}}
), by the same proof as Lemma 3.4 of
[
26
]
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Consider the setting in subsection
1.1
.
For any
\rho_{i},\rho_{i}^{\prime}\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
,

{\Lambda_{ri}},{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
,
V_{\rho_{i},{\Lambda_{ri}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho_{i}{\Lambda_{ri}}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}
,

V_{\rho_{i}^{\prime},{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho_{i}^{\prime}{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}

and
R_{i}\in\left(T_{\rho_{i}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{ri}}V_{{\rho_{i}},{\Lambda_{ri}}}},T_{\rho_{i}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime}V_{{\rho_{i}^{\prime}},{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime}}}\right)_{l}

for
i=1,2
,
we have

\displaystyle R_{1}\otimes R_{2}:=R_{1}T_{\rho_{1}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{r1}}V_{{\rho_{1}},{\Lambda_{r1}}}}\left(R_{2}\right)\in\left(T_{\rho_{1}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{r1}}V_{{\rho_{1}},{\Lambda_{r1}}}}T_{\rho_{2}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{r2}}V_{{\rho_{2}},{\Lambda_{r2}}}},T_{\rho_{1}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{r1}}^{\prime}V_{{\rho_{1}^{\prime}},{\Lambda_{r1}}^{\prime}}}T_{\rho_{2}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{r2}}^{\prime}V_{{\rho_{2}^{\prime}},{\Lambda_{r2}}^{\prime}}}\right)_{l},

(2.4)

and this defines the tensor product of intertwiners.
This tensor product satisfies following.

(i)

(R_{1}\otimes R_{2})^{*}=R_{1}^{*}\otimes R_{2}^{*}
.

(ii)

With

\rho_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
,

{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
,

V_{\rho_{i}^{\prime\prime},{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime\prime}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho_{i}^{\prime\prime}{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime\prime}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}

and
R_{i}^{\prime}\in\left(T_{\rho_{i}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime}V_{{\rho_{i}^{\prime}},{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime}}},T_{\rho_{i}^{\prime\prime}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime\prime}V_{{\rho_{i}^{\prime\prime}},{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime\prime}}}\right)_{l}

for
i=1,2
,
we have

R^{\prime}_{1}R_{1}\otimes R_{2}^{\prime}R_{2}=(R_{1}^{\prime}\otimes R_{2}^{\prime})(R_{1}\otimes R_{2})
.

(iii)

If
\rho_{i},\rho_{i}^{\prime}\in O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
,

V_{\rho_{i},{\Lambda_{ri}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho_{i}{\Lambda_{ri}}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}U}
,

V_{\rho_{i}^{\prime},{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho_{i}^{\prime}{\Lambda_{ri}}^{\prime}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}U}

and
R_{i}\in\left(T_{\rho_{i}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{r1}}V_{{\rho_{i}},{\Lambda_{r1}}}},T_{\rho_{i}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{(l)}{\Lambda_{r1}}^{\prime}V_{{\rho_{i}^{\prime}},{\Lambda_{r1}}^{\prime}}}\right)_{U}
,
then
R_{1}\otimes R_{2}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}
.

The proof is identical to that of Lemma 4.3,4.5 of
[
26
]
.

\square

Next we introduce the direct sum to
O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Consider the setting in subsection
1.1
.
Let
(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{r0})\in\mathcal{PC}
.
Assume Assumption
1.10
.
Then for any
\rho,\sigma\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
, there exist

\gamma\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
, isometries

u\in(\rho,\gamma)_{l}
,
v\in(\sigma,\gamma)_{l}

such that
uu^{*}+vv^{*}=\mathbb{I}
.
In particular,
\gamma=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits u\circ\rho+\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits v\circ\sigma
.
If
\rho,\sigma\in O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
, then

\gamma
and
u,v
can be chosen so that

\gamma\in O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
,

u\in(\rho,\gamma)_{U}
,
v\in(\sigma,\gamma)_{U}
.
If
\rho,\sigma\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
, then

\gamma
and
u,v
can be chosen so that

\gamma\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
,

u\in(\rho,\gamma)_{U}
,
v\in(\sigma,\gamma)_{U}
.

For each
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
, with
\Lambda\subset\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}
,

\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda})^{\prime\prime}
is properly infinite by Assumption
1.10
.
Therefore, there exist
isometries

u_{\Lambda},v_{\Lambda}\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda}})^{\prime\prime}\subset{\mathcal{F}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{U}
such that

u_{\Lambda}u_{\Lambda}^{*}+v_{\Lambda}v_{\Lambda}^{*}=\mathbb{I}
.

For each
{\Lambda_{r}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
, choose
{\Lambda_{{\Lambda_{r}}}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
with
({\Lambda_{{\Lambda_{r}}}},{\Lambda_{r}})\in\mathcal{PC}
.
Set
\gamma:=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits(u_{\Lambda_{0}})\rho+\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits(v_{\Lambda_{0}})\sigma
,
u:=u_{\Lambda_{0}}
,
v:=v_{\Lambda_{0}}
and

W_{{{{\Lambda_{r}}}}}:=u_{{{\Lambda}_{{\Lambda_{r}}}}}V_{\rho,{{{\Lambda_{r}}}}}u_{\Lambda_{0}}^{*}+v_{{{\Lambda}_{{\Lambda_{r}}}}}V_{\sigma,{{{\Lambda_{r}}}}}v_{\Lambda_{0}}^{*}
,
with
V_{\rho,{{{\Lambda_{r}}}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho{{{\Lambda_{r}}}}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}
,
V_{\sigma,{{{\Lambda_{r}}}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\sigma{{{\Lambda_{r}}}}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}
.
Then as in the proof of 5.7
[
26
]
, we have

\gamma\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
,
u\in(\rho,\gamma)_{U}
,
v\in(\sigma,\gamma)_{U}

and
W_{{\Lambda_{r}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho{\Lambda_{r}}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}
.

Suppose
\rho,\sigma\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
.
For any
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
, choose
\tilde{\Lambda}_{\Lambda}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
with

{\tilde{\Lambda}_{\Lambda}}\subset\Lambda\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}
.
Set

X_{\Lambda}:=u_{\tilde{\Lambda}_{\Lambda}}V_{\rho,\Lambda}u_{\Lambda_{0}}^{*}+v_{\tilde{\Lambda}_{\Lambda}}V_{\sigma,\Lambda}v_{\Lambda_{0}}^{*}

with
V_{\rho,\Lambda}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho\Lambda}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
,
V_{\sigma,\Lambda}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\sigma\Lambda}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
.
Then we have
X_{\Lambda}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma\Lambda}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
and

\gamma\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
.

\square

This defines the direct sum.
Note that it is unique up to
\simeq_{l}

(resp.
\simeq_{U}
) because
for other
\tilde{\gamma}\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}

(resp.
\tilde{\gamma}\in O^{rU}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
)

\tilde{u}\in(\rho,\tilde{\gamma})_{l}
,
\tilde{v}\in(\sigma,\tilde{\gamma})_{l}
,
(resp.
\tilde{u}\in(\rho,\tilde{\gamma})_{U}
,
\tilde{v}\in(\sigma,\tilde{\gamma})_{U}
), we have

\tilde{u}u^{*}+\tilde{v}v^{*}\in(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma})_{l}

(resp.
\tilde{u}u^{*}+\tilde{v}v^{*}\in(\gamma,\tilde{\gamma})_{U}
).

Next we prove the existence of subobject.
First we prepare several Lemmas.

Lemma 2.7
.

Consider the setting in subsection
1.1
.
Then for any
{\Lambda_{1}},{\Lambda_{2}}\subset\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}
,

\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{1}}}\right)^{\prime\prime}\cap\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{2}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}

is a factor.

Note that
{\mathcal{M}}:=\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{1}}}\right)^{\prime\prime}\cap\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{2}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}

includes
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{1}}\cap{\Lambda_{2}}}\right)^{\prime\prime}
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}\cap{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\subset\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{2}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\cap\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{1}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\cap\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{2}}\cap{\Lambda_{1}}}\right)^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}})^{\prime}={\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{I},

(2.5)

because
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
is irreducible.

\square

Lemma 2.8
.

Consider the setting in subsection
1.1
and assume Assumption
1.10
.
Let
{\Lambda_{r1}},{\Lambda_{r2}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
,
D\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
with

D\cap{\Lambda_{r1}}=\emptyset
and
D,{\Lambda_{r1}}\subset{\Lambda_{r2}}
.
Then for any projection
p\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{r1}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
,
there exists an isometry

w\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{r2}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{l}

such that
ww^{*}=p
.

We apply Lemma 5.10 of
[
26
]
.
Let
\delta>0
be the number given in Lemma 5.10 of
[
26
]
.
We also use Lemma
A.2
, Lemma
A.3
and Lemma
B.2
.
Consider the number
\delta_{3}(\delta_{2}(\delta))
given for

\delta>0
with the functions

\delta_{2}
,
\delta_{3}
given in Lemma
A.2
, Lemma
A.3
.

Let
p\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{r1}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
be a projection.
Then, because
p\in{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
, there exists
{\Lambda_{l}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{l}
,
and self-adjoint
x\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{l}}})^{\prime\prime}
such that

\left\|p-x\right\|\leq\delta_{3}(\delta_{2}(\delta))
.
Taking larger cone if necessary, we may assume

({\Lambda_{r1}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}\subset{\Lambda_{l}}
and that there exists
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}

such that

\displaystyle\Lambda\subset{\Lambda_{l}}\cap{\Lambda_{r1}}\subset{\Lambda_{l}}\cap{\Lambda_{r2}}.

(2.6)

Applying Lemma
B.2
with replacing

{\Lambda_{1}}
,
{\Lambda_{2}}
,
\Gamma
,
({\mathcal{H}},\pi)
,

,

,
\varepsilon

by

{\Lambda_{r1}}
,

,

,

,
p
,
x
,
\delta_{3}(\delta_{2}(\delta))

respectively, we obtain self-adjoint

z\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{r1}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\cap\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{l}}})^{\prime\prime}

such that
\left\|p-z\right\|\leq\delta_{3}(\delta_{2}(\delta))
.

Now apply Lemma
A.3
with

{\mathcal{H}}
,
{\mathcal{A}}
,
p
,
x

replaced by
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
,
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{r1}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\cap\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{l}}})^{\prime\prime}
,

p
,
z
respectively.
Then from Lemma
A.3
, we obtain
a projection
q\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{r1}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\cap\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{l}}})^{\prime\prime}

such that

.

Next we apply Lemma
A.2
with

{\mathcal{A}}
,
p
,
q
replaced by

\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{r1}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
,
p
,
q
respectively.
Then from Lemma
A.2
, we obtain a unitary

u\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{r1}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{l}

such that
p=uqu^{*}
,
\left\|u-\mathbb{I}\right\|<\delta
.

From Lemma
2.7
,
{\mathcal{M}}:=\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{r2}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\cap\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{{\Lambda_{l}}})^{\prime\prime}

is a factor.
For the cone
\Lambda\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
satisfying (
2.6
),

\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda})^{\prime\prime}
is an infinite factor from Assumption
1.10
.
Because
{\mathcal{M}}
includes
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\Lambda})^{\prime\prime}
, it means

{\mathcal{M}}
is an infinite factor.

Now we apply Lemma 5.10 of
[

]
, with

{\mathcal{H}}
,

,

,

,

replaced by

,

,

,

,

.
Then from Lemma 5.10 of
[

]
, we have

q\sim\mathbb{I}
in
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Namely, there exists an isometry
v\in{\mathcal{M}}

such that

.

Set
w:=uv\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{r2}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
.
Then this
w
is isometry with

, proving the claim.

\square

Now we are ready to introduce subobjects.

Lemma 2.9
.

Consider the setting in subsection
1.1
and assume Assumption
1.10
.
Let
(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{r0})\in\mathcal{PC}
.
Then for any
\rho\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
and a projection
p\in(\rho,\rho)_{l}
, there exists
\gamma\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}

and an isometry
v\in(\gamma,\rho)_{l}

such that
vv^{*}=p
.
In particular,
\gamma=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits v^{*}\rho
.

For any
{\Lambda_{r}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
, fix
V_{\rho,{\Lambda_{r}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\rho{\Lambda_{r}}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}
and set

p_{{\Lambda_{r}}}:=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits(V_{\rho,{\Lambda_{r}}})(p)
.
Then as in the proof of Lemma 5.8
[
26
]
,

p_{{\Lambda_{r}}}
is a projection in
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{r}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
.

For each
{\Lambda_{r}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
, fix
\Gamma_{{\Lambda_{r}}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
,
D_{{\Lambda_{r}}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}

such that
D_{{\Lambda_{r}}}\cap\Gamma_{{\Lambda_{r}}}=\emptyset
and

D_{{\Lambda_{r}}},\Gamma_{{\Lambda_{r}}}\subset{\Lambda_{r}}
.
By Lemma
2.8
, for the projection

p_{{\Gamma_{{\Lambda_{r}}}}}\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{(\Gamma_{{\Lambda_{r}}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{l}
, there exists
an isometry
w_{{\Lambda_{r}}}\in\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{({\Lambda_{r}})^{c}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{H}_{U}}}\right)^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{l}

such that
w_{{\Lambda_{r}}}w_{{\Lambda_{r}}}^{*}=p_{\Gamma_{{\Lambda_{r}}}}
.
We set
\gamma:=\mathop{\mathrm{Ad}}\nolimits\left(w_{\Lambda_{r0}}^{*}V_{\rho,\Gamma_{\Lambda_{r0}}}\right)\circ\rho
,

X_{{\Lambda_{r}}}:=w_{{\Lambda_{r}}}^{*}V_{\rho,\Gamma_{{\Lambda_{r}}}}V_{\rho,\Gamma_{\Lambda_{r0}}}^{*}w_{\Lambda_{r0}}
,

v:=V_{\rho,\Gamma_{\Lambda_{r0}}}^{*}w_{\Lambda_{r0}}
.
Then, as in the proof of Lemma 5.8
[
26
]
,
we get

\gamma\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}

X_{{\Lambda_{r}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\gamma{\Lambda_{r}}}^{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}

and
v\in(\gamma,\rho)_{l}
is an isometry
such that
vv^{*}=p
.

\square

This defines the subobjects.
Note that it is unique up to
\simeq_{l}
because
for other
\tilde{\gamma}\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
,

\tilde{v}\in(\gamma,\rho)_{l}
,
v^{*}\tilde{v}\in(\tilde{\gamma},\gamma)_{l}
.
Hence we obtain the following.

Theorem 2.10
.

Consider the setting in subsection
1.1
and assume
Assumption
1.10
.
Let
\Lambda_{r0}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}}^{r}
.
Let
C^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
be the category with objects
O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
and morphisms

(\rho,\sigma)_{l}
between each
\rho,\sigma\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
.
Then
C^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
is the
C^{*}
-tensor category with respect to the
tensor structure
\otimes
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\rho\otimes\sigma:=\rho\circ_{{\Lambda_{r0}}}\sigma,\quad\rho,\sigma\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}},\\
&R\otimes S:=RT_{\rho}^{\mathrm{(l)}\Lambda_{r0}\mathbb{I}}(S),\quad R\in(\rho,\rho^{\prime})_{l},\quad S\in(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})_{l},\quad\rho,\rho^{\prime},\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}.\end{split}

(2.7)

The representation
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}\in O^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
is the tensor unit,
with
\mathbb{I}
as the associativity isomorphisms as well as
left and right multiplication by the tensor unit
\pi_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}}}
.

Analogous to Lemma
2.8
and Lemma
2.9
, we have the followings.

Lemma 2.11
.

Consider the setting in subsection
1.1
and assume Assumption
1.10
.
Let
{\Lambda_{1}},{\Lambda_{2}}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathop{\mathrm{bk}}}}^{U}
,

with

and

,

such that

.

Lemma 2.12
.

Consider the setting in subsection
1.1
and assume Assumption
1.10
.

(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{r0})\in\mathcal{PC}
.

.

Theorem 2.13
.

Consider the setting in subsection
1.1
and assume
Assumption
1.10
.
Let
(\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{r0})\in\mathcal{PC}
.
Let
C_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
be the category with objects
O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
and morphisms

(\rho,\sigma)_{U}
between each
\rho,\sigma\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
.
Then
C_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
is a
C^{*}
-tensor subcategory of
C^{r}_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd},\Lambda_{r0}}}
in the following sense:

(i)

C_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
is closed under
*
, composition of morphisms, and tensor.

(ii)

Direct sum
\gamma
,
u\in(\rho,\gamma)_{l}
,

v\in(\sigma,\gamma)_{l}
of
\rho,\sigma\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
can be taken so that

\gamma\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
,
u\in(\rho,\gamma)_{U}
,

v\in(\sigma,\gamma)_{U}
.

(iii)

For any
\rho\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
and projection
p\in(\rho,\rho)_{U}
,
subobject
\gamma
,
v\in(\gamma,\rho)_{l}
can be taken so that
\gamma\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}

and
v\in(\gamma,\rho)_{U}
.

For any
\rho,\sigma\in O_{\mathop{\mathrm{bd}},\Lambda_{0}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{bu}}}
,
(\rho,\sigma)_{U}\subset(\rho,\sigma)_{l}
, and (i) is
(iv) of Lemma
2.4
.
(ii) is proven in Lemma
2.6
.(iii) is Lemma
2.12
.

\square
